.
For a finite set of morphisms
S
, we also write
\operatorname{gr}(S)
for the set of graphs.

Lemma 58
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an ordered DAG and let
S\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}
be a set of generators.
Then, in
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
, we have
\operatorname{gr}(\operatorname{Cpy}(S))=\operatorname{Cmp}(\operatorname{gr}(S))
.

In the statement we explicitly mention
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
to point out that we cannot consider the case of CD-categories, where the compare-composition is not defined.
The proof is by induction, and it follows from the special Frobenius equations (
2
).

Remark 59
.

For each ordered DAG
\mathcal{G}
, we have a CD-functor
\star_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
given by sending each generator to itself (recall that
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\mathsf{FreeHyp}(V_{\mathcal{G}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}})
).
In particular, the family
(\star_{\mathcal{G}})_{\mathcal{G}}
is a natural transformation when we intepret
\mathsf{CDSyn}
and
\mathsf{HSyn}
as functors
{\mathsf{ODAG}}\to{\mathsf{CDcat}}
.

As already stated in the main text, any semantics CD-functor
F\colon\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathsf{FinStoch}
yields a hypergraph functor
\tilde{F}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})
defined on generators in the same way as
F
.
We note that by definition, this modification satisfies the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}}

With the same strategy, we define
\tilde{!}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
, the hypergraph functor such that
\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\star}\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\tilde{!}_{\mathcal{G}}}\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
coincides with
\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{!_{\mathcal{G}}}\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{\star_{\mathcal{G}}}\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

32
.

To ensure that the moralisation gives rise to a functor, we first prove that
\tilde{!}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
factors through
m\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Recall that
m
, defined in (
6
), sends
C
to the graph
v
\operatorname{Pa}(v)
whenever
C=\{v\}\cup\operatorname{Pa}(v)
, and to
otherwise.

We study the composition
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{!_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}}\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}\xrightarrow{m}\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
By definition,
!_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}
sends the unique generator
I\to\bullet
to
\operatorname{Cmp}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})})
.
Before discussing how
m
acts on
\operatorname{Cmp}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})})
, since the moralisation connects parents,
\{v\}\cup\operatorname{Pa}(v)
is a clique in
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})
for every
v\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
, i.e. all
v
\operatorname{Pa}(v)
are in the image of
m
.
We conclude that
m(\operatorname{Cmp}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}))=\operatorname{Cmp}(\operatorname{gr}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}))
, because the compare morphisms are preserved by hypergraph functors. By
Lemma

58
,
\operatorname{Cmp}(\operatorname{gr}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}))=\operatorname{gr}(\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}))
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}})
has trivial input, so it coincides with its graph:
\operatorname{Cmp}(\operatorname{gr}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}}))=\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}})
.
On the other side,
\tilde{!}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}
sends
I\to\bullet
to
\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}})
, so the wanted factorisation holds.

Now, whenever we have a Bayesian network
(\omega,\mathcal{G})
, consider some
F\colon\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathsf{FinStoch}
factorising
\omega
. Then the associated Markov network
(\omega,\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))
is obtained by the commutative diagram (already appeared in the main text)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinProjStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinStoch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{!}_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

(10)

where we used
Remark

59
as well as the fact that
\mathsf{FinStoch}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})\xrightarrow{q}\mathsf{FinProjStoch}
coincides with
i
by direct check.

We are left to discuss how the moralisation acts on morphisms.
Let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
be an order-preserving graph homomorphism.
Then
\alpha
can be seen as an order-preserving graph homomorphism
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})
, as stated in the main text.
This is easily checked: every edge
v\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w
in
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
either comes from an edge of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, so it is obviosly preserved by
\alpha
, or it is given by a certain vertex
u
such that
v\to u
and
w\to u
. Since
\alpha
sends edges to edges,
\alpha(v)
and
\alpha(w)
are parents (or coincide with)
\alpha(u)
.
This means that we have an edge
\alpha(v)\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}\alpha(w)
in
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})
(or
\alpha(v)=\alpha(w)
).
We can then define the moralisation functor on morphisms by sending
(\alpha,\eta)\colon(\omega,\mathcal{G})\to(\omega^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
to
(\alpha,\eta)\colon(\omega,\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))\to(\omega^{\prime},\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

34
.

Analogously to the previous proof, we start by showing that
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}
factors through
t\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}
as defined in (
8
).

By definition,
\tilde{!}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}
sends the unique generator
I\to\bullet
to the copy-composition
\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})})
.
As we noted in the previous proof,
\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})})=\operatorname{gr}(\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}))
because it has trivial input. Therefore, by
Lemma

58
,
\operatorname{Cpy}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})})=\operatorname{Cmp}(\operatorname{gr}(\Sigma_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}))
.
The image of this morphism via
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}
is then given by
\operatorname{Cmp}(\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}\operatorname{Cmp}(C_{v}))
, where
C_{v}=\{\phi\colon I\to C\mid v\in C\subseteq\operatorname{Pa}(v)\cup\{v\}\}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Here, the additional tensoring seemingly needed with
Q
(where
Q
is defined as in (
8
)) can be disregarded because for every vertex
v
, the fact that
\{v\}\in C_{v}
ensures that
v
is always considered in
\operatorname{Cmp}(\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}\operatorname{Cmp}(C_{v}))
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Cmp}(\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}\operatorname{Cmp}(C_{v}))=\operatorname{Cmp}(\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}C_{v})
by
Lemma

52
because the sets
C_{v}
are all disjoint: if a clique
D
belongs to
C_{v}\cap C_{w}
, then
v,w\in D
and both of them are parents of each other, which contradicts the fact that
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})
is a DAG.

We claim that
\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}{C_{v}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}
, where
\subseteq
holds by definition.
Let us write
\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}{C_{v}}
more explicitly:

\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}{C_{v}}=\left\{\phi\colon I\to C\,|\,\exists\,v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\text{ such that }v\in C\subseteq\operatorname{Pa}(v)\cup\{v\}\right\}.

Now, given any clique
C\in C\ell(\mathcal{H})
, let us consider the biggest element
v\in C
.
Then for any element
w\in C
, there must exist an edge
w\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}v
because
C
is a clique.
By assumption,
w\leq v
, and therefore
w\to v
in
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})
.
We conclude that
v\in C\subseteq\operatorname{Pa}(v)\cup\{v\}
, so
\phi\colon I\to C
belongs to
C_{v}
.
As claimed,
\bigcup_{v\in V_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}{C_{v}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}}
, and therefore

\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}
sends
I\to\bullet
to
\operatorname{Cmp}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{H}})
.
Since
!_{\mathcal{H}}\colon\mathsf{HSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}
is defined by the same assignment, we have shown that
!_{\mathcal{H}}
indeed factorises through
t
.
Finally, any
(\omega,\mathcal{H})\in{\mathsf{MN}}
leads to
\omega
being factored through
\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}
in the following way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinProjStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

However, we want to show that this is sufficient to ensure the existence of
\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})}\to\mathsf{FinStoch}
factorising
\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}\to\mathsf{FinStoch}
. This is the content of
Lemma

60
below, which therefore allows us to say that the triangulation sends
(\omega,\mathcal{H})\in{\mathsf{MN}}
to
(\omega,\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}))\in{\mathsf{BN}}
.

As in the proof of
Theorem

32
, to describe how triangulation acts on morphisms, we first prove that every order-preserving graph homomorphism
\alpha\colon\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{H}
can be interpreted as an order-preserving graph homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})
.
The fact that the graph homomorphism is order-preserving ensures that this is true, indeed whenever
v\to w
in
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
, then there exists a path
v\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w_{1}\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}\dots\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w_{n}=w
with
w_{i}\geq w
. As both of these properties are preserved by
\alpha
, the same is true for
\alpha(v)
and
\alpha(w)
, and therefore they either coincide or there exists a directed edge
\alpha(v)\to\alpha(w)
, as wanted.
This ensures that the triangulation functor can be defined on morphisms by sending
(\alpha,\eta)\colon(\omega,\mathcal{H})\to(\omega^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
to
(\alpha,\eta)\colon(\omega,\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}))\to(\omega^{\prime},\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}))
.
∎

The ordered DAG
\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})
is such that for all vertices
u,v,w\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
,

u,v\to w\text{ and }u\leq v\qquad\implies\qquad u\to v.

(11)

This is immediate as
u,v\to w
ensures two paths with
w

u\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w_{1}\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}\dots\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w_{n}=w=w^{\prime}_{m}\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}\dots\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}w^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\rule[2.15277pt]{10.00002pt}{0.2pt}}v,

where
w_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\geq w
, so
u\to v
.

Lemma 60
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an ordered DAG satisfying (
11
).
If the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{HSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FinProjStoch}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star_{\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

commutes, where
F
is a hypergraph functor, then there exists a factorisation
\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{!_{\mathcal{G}}}\mathsf{CDSyn}_{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{G}\mathsf{FinStoch}
of
\omega
for some CD-functor
G
.

This result is the only one that uses tools from the standard theory.
However, the proof can be modified and rewritten from the viewpoint of categorical probability by using the notions of conditionals and normalisations
[
7
,
18
]
.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, we use
Remark

42
, as well as the discussion in the proof of
Proposition

24
on page
9
, to translate the statement to a factorisation of probability distributions.
In this way, the statement reads as follows: Let
\mathcal{G}
be an ordered DAG satisfying (
11
) and consider a probability distribution
\omega
and a factorisation
\omega(V_{\mathcal{G}})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{v\in V_{\mathcal{G}}}f_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))
, where
f_{v}
belongs to
\mathsf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0})
. Then
\omega
admits a factorisation
\omega=\prod_{v\in V_{\mathcal{G}}}g_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))
where
g_{v}
is a stochastic matrix.

We proceed by induction. Let us assume that the statement holds true for all graphs with
n-1
vertices, and prove it for graphs with
n
vertices.

Let us take the biggest element
v\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
and consider

\lambda_{v}(\operatorname{Pa}(v))\coloneqq\sum_{x\in v}f_{v}(x\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))\quad\text{ and }\quad g_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{f_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))}{\lambda_{v}(\operatorname{Pa}(v))}&\text{if }\lambda_{v}(\operatorname{Pa}(v))\neq 0\\
\operatorname{unif}_{v}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\operatorname{unif}_{v}
is the uniform probability on
v
(this can actually be substituted by any probability distribution).
In particular, with this choice
g_{v}
is a stochastic matrix, i.e. it belongs to
\mathsf{FinStoch}
.

We note that
f_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))=\lambda_{v}(\operatorname{Pa}(v))g_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))
.
By assumption, if we take the biggest parent
w
of
v
, then
\operatorname{Pa}(v)\setminus\{w\}\subseteq\operatorname{Pa}(w)
.
In particular, we can define

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{w}(w\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(w))\coloneqq\lambda_{v}(\operatorname{Pa}(v))f_{w}(w\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(w)),\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{u}(u\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(u))\coloneqq f_{u}(u\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(u))\qquad\text{for all }u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{v,w\}

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by deleting
v
. From the factorisation
\omega(V_{\mathcal{G}})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{u\in V_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}f^{\prime}_{u}(u\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(u))g_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))
, a direct check shows that
\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{u\in V_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}f^{\prime}_{u}(u\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(u))
is equal to the marginalization
\omega^{\prime}
of
\omega
.
Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
satisfies (
11
) because
\mathcal{G}
does, we can apply the induction hypothesis to show that
\omega^{\prime}
can be factorised by stochastic matrices.
Since
g_{v}(v\,|\,\operatorname{Pa}(v))
is also a stochastic matrix, the statement is also true for
\omega
, as wanted.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

37
.

The two natural transformations are simply achieved by noting that the identity on an ordered DAG
\mathcal{G}
and an ordered undirected graph
\mathcal{H}
are also morphisms
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))
and
\mathcal{H}\to\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H}))
.
That these correspond to natural transformation follows because the functors
\operatorname{Mor}(-)
and
\operatorname{Tr}(-)
send the morphism
(\alpha,\eta)
to the same pair intepreted in the new type, as stated at the end of the proofs of
Theorems

32
and
34
.
∎

Proposition 61
.

There is no adjunction given by
\operatorname{Mor}(-)
and
\operatorname{Tr}(-)
.

Proof.

By contradiction, let us assume that a natural transformation
\mu_{(\omega,\mathcal{G})}\colon(\omega,\mathcal{G})\to(\omega,\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})))
exists.
Recall that the functors
\operatorname{Mor}(-)
and
\operatorname{Tr}(-)
are ‘identity-like’, i.e. they send
(\alpha,\eta)
to
(\alpha,\eta)
intepreted in the new type.
For any
(\omega,\mathcal{G})
and any vertex
v\in\mathcal{G}
, let us consider
(\omega_{v},\bullet)
where
\omega_{v}
is the marginalization of
\omega
at
v
.
The marginalization gives rise to a distribution-preserving monoidal transformation
\pi\colon\omega\to\omega_{v}
, so in particular we have a morphism
(i,\pi)\colon(\omega,\mathcal{G})\to(\omega_{v},\bullet)
where
i
is the inclusion of graphs
\bullet\to\mathcal{G}
sending
\bullet
to
v
.
By assumption,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega_{v},\bullet)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega_{v},\bullet)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega,\mathcal{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega,\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G})))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(\omega_{v},\bullet)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(\omega,\mathcal{G})}}

commutes.
At the level of graphs,
\mu_{(\omega_{v},\bullet)}
must be given by the identity, as it is the only morphism
\bullet\to\bullet
.
Therefore, whatever
\alpha\colon\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))\to\mathcal{G}
represents
\eta_{(\omega,\mathcal{G})}
at the level of graphs, it must respect the commutation of the diagram above, which means that
\alpha(v)=v
.
The arbitrariety of
v
implies that
\alpha
must be the identity, but the identity is not necessarily a graph homomorphisms
\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))\to\mathcal{G}
because
\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))
has in general more edges than
\mathcal{G}
.
For the sake of an example, let
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq{\hbox to37.6pt{\vbox to9.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.51224pt\lower-4.6347pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \hbox to0.0pt{

, and note that
\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{G}))
is a complete DAG (i.e. we have the additional edge
A\to B
).

The same idea applies when assuming the existence of a natural transformation
(\omega,\mathcal{H})\to(\omega,\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{Tr}(\mathcal{H})))
, and therefore
\operatorname{Mor}(-)
and
\operatorname{Tr}(-)
do not admit a unit for the possible adjunction in either direction.
∎
